ParlaMint-NO_2012-06-12.ana.ud2618e177	Honourable co-representatives! Former Parliamentary representative and parliamentary leader, Harald Synnes, died on 17 May, 81 years old. Harald Synnes represented Vest-Agder Christian People's Party on the Stortinget. For twelve years from 1969 he was an alternate before being permanently elected as a representative from 1981 to 1989. With him, a committed, willful and strongly ideologically anchored politician has gone away. Harald Synnes was born on 18 March 1931 in Bø, Vesterålen. After completing cand.real education, he settled in Kristiansand, where he worked as a lecturer. He came into local politics early. In 1960 he became a member of Kristiansand City Council. For 20 years he has had strong local political involvement, as rapporteur, rapporteur and member of the county council. In his time at Stortinget, Synnes was a member of the Finance Committee, except for one year, from 1985 to 1986, when he was a member of the Foreign and Constitutional Committee. He was also a member of the enlarged Foreign and Constitutional Committee and of the delegation to the Nordic Council. When the Christian People's Party joined the Willoch government in 1983, he became the parliamentary leader. Harald Synnes also had experience with the government apparatus. In the Korvald government he was State Secretary at the Prime Minister's office, and he became a central person in the negotiations for a trade agreement with the EC. In the Syse government, he was State Secretary at the Ministry of Culture and the Church. Harald Synnes had a number of responsibilities. He was, among other things, Member of the Joint Council, of the Human Rights Committee, of the Board of No to the EU and Chairman of the Regional Council of Agder. In recent years he was the chairman and the work of the Arctic Course and Competence Centre. Harald Synnes was a capable and knowledgeable person with great work capacity. He strongly engaged in the fight for the disadvantaged and actively working in Stortinget, among others. for a fairer tax system and for relief measures to disadvantaged pregnant women. He was, both as a person and as a politician, one who always stood up for what he meant and believed in. The EEA matter became difficult and led to him for some time withdrawing from the Christian People's Party, but he came back and made a great effort as the leader of the party's senior selection. We remember Harald Synnes for his work in the Storting and shine peace over his memory. The representatives heard the President's commemorative speech.

ParlaMint-NO_2012-06-12.ana.ud2618e272	The matter we are now dealing with is a joint proposal from the Ministry of Finance with proposals for amendments to the Accounting Act, the Securities Trading Act, the Real Estate Act, etc. The Committee broadly endorses the proposals made in the proposal in most areas, but is divided into the view on the length of retention of documents required by the Accounting Act. I would like to link some comments to that very question and comment on the proposal for changes to the real estate law. As regards the length of the retention period for documents under the Accounting Act, the majority of the Committee takes note that the Government is not now proposing a change to the requirement of a 10-year retention period for primary documents covered by the Accounting Act. This is in line with the recommendations of the Tax Directorate and Ecokrim and is supported by a large majority of the hearing bodies including the Ministry of Health and Welfare, the Ministry of Justice, the Ministry of Knowledge, the Nav, the Competition Authority, the LO, the Auditor’s Association, the Riksadvokaten and the Sparebank Association. In the majority’s view, the long-term background to the documents is that they contribute to ensuring evidence as long as there may be a real need to verify them. In identifying and investigating organised economic crime, this is essential, and the majority have taken note of the Ecocrime's clear recommendation in the case, where they just point out that the retention period has had an impact both in general and in a number of examples on the fight against just financial crime. The majority also suggests that there must be consistency between the requirement to keep documents and the limitation period in tax and criminal matters. The majority are at the same time concerned with their objective of business simplification, as we were when, for example: removed the obligation to audit the annual accounts of the smallest companies. The key issue of retention is whether the use of the ten-year retention requirement is proportionate to the costs incurred by the accounting officer. The minority in the Committee points out in its comments the costs they consider to be incurred by business in requiring ten years' retention of accounting documents and proposes to reduce them to five years. This is supported by several consultation bodies, such as the Data Protection Agency, HSH, Narf and NHO. The rationale for minority views and proposals will be addressed by others. However, the Committee agrees that current paper documents will gradually be replaced by other storage media and is in favour of clarifying that there is freedom of choice in relation to storage medium. The Committee considers that this will contribute to the reduction of storage costs for business. The second amendment I want to make a few comments to is the Property Agents Act. When the law on real estate brokerage was passed here in the Stortinget in 2007, the significant changes were made in comparison with the previous law. Among other things, the skills requirements of those working as brokers were strengthened. The law is also adjusted in this case, by allowing the staff to work for a certain period without passing the examination as an estate agent in regulatory homes. Such a scheme will, at the discretion of the Ministry, meet a need for greater flexibility in the recruitment of assistants in the real estate brokerage industry, while assisting before the examination will be able to gain relevant knowledge and experience. The Committee points out that the intention of the examination is to increase the level of competence in the industry and therefore considers it essential that the new home does not lead to the widespread use of temporary employment of non-examination assistants. In addition to these comments, the Committee endorses the proposal. The minority will take up their proposal. I hereby submit the matter.
ParlaMint-NO_2012-06-12.ana.ud2618e298	The length of time required for accounting officers to keep records depends, of course, on weighing many different considerations. I'm glad that case no. 2 and 3 will be treated together. The right's view is only on the holding of accounts and not the other on case No. 2 – which we otherwise support. The retention period requirement shall ensure that the documentation is available to the authorities for as long as there may be a need to verify it. At the same time, the requirement to keep costs for those subject to bookkeeping shall mean that the retention period should be no longer than strictly necessary. The right considers that Norway should follow the developments that have occurred in Denmark and Sweden and that we should reduce the retention period to five years. On the basis of the material currently available, the Ministry has, for some reason, come to the conclusion that further monitoring of the costs related to storage time requirements is necessary, before any proposal to the Storting House to reduce storage time can be made. In addition, the Ministry does not propose any changes to the current retention period requirements, despite many reports, extensive consultation opinions – on which the case-rapporteur was involved – from an overall business life and not least a unanimous opposition, as well as the government's own concrete objective of reducing administrative burdens on business. The right is therefore surprised that the government is unable to take a stand on a matter in which the consequences are clearly more than well established. On the basis of the Stortingen’s request, the Ministry asked both the Tax Directorate and the Økokrim at the end of 2007 for an assessment of the 10 year retention requirement for primary documentation. The right therefore believes that the government must start work on any changes in the law, so that progress is maintained in this work, which has obviously stopped completely under this government. In Denmark, as a general rule, a five-year retention period is required. Some exceptions have been made. In Sweden, retention time was reduced from ten to seven years as part of the Swedish government’s simplification work. The right notes that the simplification work for business has better conditions in our neighbouring countries, which at the time the changes were adopted, both had bourgeois governments. Both the Right and the Government have promised business simplifications of 5 billion. kr during 2013. It is very difficult for the government to do so if it is unable to implement the most obvious measures, such as: the proposal to halve the retention obligation. The government itself writes that the costs associated with the retention of accounting material — which, in the view of the government, should be assessed further — are estimated at 631.5 million. kr per year. So it's 2.2 pst. of the total administrative costs of the enterprises. A follow-up report on the development of the administrative costs of business, prepared by Rambøll Management in November 2009 for the government, indicates that nothing has happened in the period 2006 to 2009. In the consultation response to Narf, it is shown, inter alia, that: to keep the industry stored at all times around 80 million book covers, and that it is only in 1 pst. by the enterprises being checked, errors are found in the material. That means, based on the Narf's way of counting, that 40 million book covers every single year are kept almost to no avail, and this is estimated to cost 260 million. kr for business. The right cannot see that the cost and additional work are proportionate to the benefits of the legislation. The right considers that the government’s opinions on the need for further monitoring are not justified by the lack of information, but simply by the lack of decision-making and internal disagreement within the government. Therefore, it is unfortunate that the necessary simplification work for business is at best delayed and, in the worst case, stopped once again. The government has claimed that an external investigation could be completed at the end of the year, so the Right assumes that the case will be dealt with positively in the context of the revised state budget for 2013. The Right, the Progressive Party, the Christian People's Party and the Left have promoted joint proposals both as a proposal for a representative and as part of case No. 2nd@item: inlistbox I want to vote in both cases and address both proposals.

ParlaMint-NO_2012-06-12.ana.ud2618e519	The starting point for the Left's tax policy is that it should be profitable to work, to save, to invest in Norwegian enterprises and jobs, and not least to contribute to environmental and climate-friendly societal development. Therefore, the core of the Left's tax policy is a shift from red to green tax. With the tax policy of the Left, working and creating values will be more profitable, living environmentally friendly, but pollution will be less profitable. The left wants to facilitate an innovative business, where innovation and development in existing enterprises are important, and how many start their own business. The left will therefore facilitate better relationships for individual enterprises, not least for better and equal social rights. Various government councils have repeatedly said at the request of the Left that they will consider strengthening the social rights of self-employed people. Nothing has happened so far. Last week, the Stortinget dealt with a 400-page report from the Norwegian Government on employment policy. Self-employed persons are not mentioned in a word. The left has a different starting point. That is why we have a number of proposals in this position that will improve the conditions of the self-employed, from better social rights, including sick pay, to increased maximum savings in the occupational pension scheme to better and more sensible rules regarding the payment of taxes. Let me stop a little bit at the last. On May 28th this year, author Tom Egeland sent out the following Twitter message: ‘I exceeded the deadline for advance tax by a few days. Thus, I have to pay the rest of the year's tax NOW." Yes, this is actually the law for the self-employed. Not only do they have to pay taxes on a quarterly basis on the basis of an assumed income. If you are one or two days late, it is easy to assume that you have to pay the entire estimated tax you would have to have on an assumed income for the whole year. Many have to borrow to pay taxes on an assumed income. For this reason, today, the Left promotes a proposal to change the tax collection rules for self-employed workers in order to bring them closer to the usual taxpayers. A short time ago, there was an interesting interpellation here in the Stortinget, SVs Geir-Ketil Hansen raised the question of using fiscal policy actively in order to achieve the Stortinget's agreed goal in health policy. Hansen called for an active fee policy in the case of foodstuffs: a higher charge on unhealthy foods, a lower charge on healthy foods. This is entirely in agreement with the Left. That is why we are proposing a conversion of VAT on food in line with Hansen's call: no VAT on fruit and vegetables, full VAT on shower and other highly sugary beverages. This view was supported in the interpellation debate by the representative Lundteigen, who appealed to the Minister of Finance to have a full focus on the VAT system for beverages. I can't stand to quote what he said in the hall, but I hope he repeats it now, because he comes as the next speaker, I see. The paradoxical and regrettable thing is that both the SV and the Central Party are voting against the policy they are calling for. I would, however, like to congratulate the SV and the Central Party in the hope of seeing evidence of this in the policy of the 2013 budget. While the Left will improve business conditions, we will stimulate business to become more climate-friendly and environmentally friendly. Through the tax system, it can be done in two ways – either by increasing environmental taxes or by introducing differentiated rates that premium desired environmental and climate-friendly behaviour. The left therefore proposes to introduce its own environmental depreciation rate in balancing group d, so that a further 5 pst depreciation is possible, if the investment clearly generates an environmental and/or climate benefit. The same approach is followed by the Left in the case of the TAX FUNN scheme. Today it's only 10 pst. of the research activity within the TaxFUNN that can be related to climate, environment and energy efficiency improvement. This will increase Left and therefore proposes the introduction of own funds limits in addition to such R & D activity, by 2 million and 4 million respectively. kr, depending on whether the research has been carried out internally in the enterprise or externally. Finally, I take up the Left's proposal in the setting, with the hope that the government will have a sloppy look at these at the next state budget.

ParlaMint-NO_2012-06-12.ana.ud2618e553	The cases relate to changes in different laws relating to the revised national budget, and I have some comments, first of all to the rules of procedure in property taxes. The tax on property is municipal, and what is a difficult question is whether to give municipalities the right of transparency and to complain when determining the tax base for power plants. In principle, it is wrong that the person who is a taxpayer does not have full access rights, and the property tax is, as I said, municipal. We are then in a situation where it is the Central Tax Office for such matters that handle this, and it has subsequently proved to be significant errors, and therefore the need has indicated that the person who is the taxpayer is fully transparent. It is important to follow further in this case, because it is central to maintaining trust between the municipalities, which have made the natural resources available in the context of power development, and thus have the right and the requirement for a part of the value creation, and the big community, which receives the main benefit from such development. It is important to have confidence and trust that taxes are properly charged. As far as property taxes are concerned, the opposition proposes changes related to property taxes on machinery and easily movable equipment. This is a proposal from the NHO, and it is a requirement that we are increasingly seeing coming, based on the competitive situation of the mainland industry. According to the views of the government and the Central Party, it is wrong to ship by the system that has been here, because integrated machinery and accessories are part of the real estate. If you don't keep the integrated machinery and accessories, it's no longer a work or a use, then it's a building. Therefore, this goes to the core of property taxation for works and uses. A different interface than what we have today is hard to imagine. It's a 100-year-old case law that is related to what we have today, and if you start with something else, we get a number of disputes. For example, we might ask, what is easily removable equipment, which is referred to here? Yes, it depends on what kind of machinery you have. All equipment can be moved today. It can be dismantled in suitable sizes, so it can be moved. We also need to be aware that the property tax, which is municipal, is to be deducted from the companies in the state corporation tax. We have to get that whole thing out. As regards the strengthening of the legal certainty of taxpayers, there has been a debate in the past, on which several have been involved. We now get a reduced additional tax of 10 pst. when taxpayers have provided incorrect or incomplete information on items in the declaration, which they are given unannounced by the employer or other third parties. The government is now following up the big debate in this area, and I am pleased with the interaction we have between Stortinget and the Ministry of Finance – it is developing well in a number of areas. On the issue of the reverse charge obligation, VAT, in the construction industry, which means that the purchaser will be able to charge the charges – unlike today – there has been a thorough debate and discussion on this issue, both in the government parties and not least in the civil service, and the proposal has been rejected. I hope that discussion is now dead, as I hear that the opposition says they're just asking for a simpler solution. This question is so complicated that if you want to do it in a good way, it is difficult to imagine a simpler solution – it is more complicated. As far as other proposals are concerned, the Progress Party has said in an exemplary way that they do not wish to re-examine these issues raised earlier in the revised national budget. I want to mention a couple of points. With regard to the proposal for a write-off, made by the Left and the Advance Party, I would say that depreciation rules should reflect the impairment, and that principle must be adhered to. There are a number of points where the impairment is greater than the depreciation today, and it must have the main focus. The Land Interest Tax Proposal from the Right to increase the minimum limit from 5500 kVA to 11000 kVA only represents a new barrier and does not change the problem in principle. The problems can only be solved by having a linear degression from where you have zero in the rent tax, to where you get a full rent tax. Furthermore, the view of the Left on full VAT on sugar-containing drinks has been fully supported by the Centre Party, whether the sugar is naturally or artificially added.
ParlaMint-NO_2012-06-12.ana.ud2618e579	The Progress Party has always taken the view of the revised national budget that it is not a new budgetary procedure. The reason for the additional tax is that it contributes to the compliance of taxpayers with the disclosure obligation under the law. The Party of Progress considers that the justification cannot be used in the same way when the authorities already have the information in question. That is why we are promoting the following proposals: ‘The government is asked to propose the introduction of a maximum amount of additional tax in cases where the authorities already hold the information when it is reported from other sources. The maximum amount is set at NOK 10000.’ The government also assesses the reverse charge obligation in the construction industry. We believe that the Ministry of Finance, in consultation with industry, should look at different models – and not just reject them – to see if we can find a simpler solution than the working group has proposed. Such a proposal could be put to the consultation so that the proposal would be dealt with more closely by the government than it has been proposed. I'm taking up our suggestions.

ParlaMint-NO_2012-06-12.ana.ud2618e3944	There is no doubt that, as usual, government accounts are comprehensive and provide a solid insight into Norwegian government finances. That's $4.9 billion. kr lower than in the newly aged budget, adopted last December. If we proceed to our money on the book, we see that the book value of the Staten pension fund abroad at the end of 2011 was NOK 3 307.9 billion. That's almost 230 billion. kr more than at the end of 2010. We also have the State pension fund Norway. Here the figures show that from the end of 2010 until the end of 2011 the capital has decreased by 5.4 billion. kr, and must be seen as a result of a somewhat disrupted year in the stock and interest market. Nevertheless, in 2011, overall, revenue and expenditure of the nation went better than expected — or feared — if you like. In particular, industrial taxes and levies have been higher than estimated by last year's budget decision for a total of 40 billion. kr more. An application for income is made without a change in a tax rule. The farms in this country are doing just better than expected. We should be grateful for that. Both the natural resources and the Norwegian labour market mean that we have many jobs that contribute significant income, which, as the accounts show, benefits the community. But wealth also requires that we take responsibility and that we manage the values well, so that future generations can also benefit from them. With all due respect, almost everyone can do it. What is demanding is to hold back — consider what is being done with the best professional estimates — when you do not know what the economic crisis, the terrain, will look like, when you have to take into account uncertainty, and when you have to take possible developments into account. There's no art to tip what's been. It's a lot more demanding to tap into what things are going to be like. Here it would have been possible – what to say – to engage in a major political debate on major issues, but I have no intention of contributing to it, except to point out that there are more things that make the Norwegian national accounts a good professional money management. The figures also show that we have a large impact on the expenditure side, but we are bombing quite heavily on the income side. ParlaMint-NO_2012-06-12.ana.ud2618e4108	I'm sorry about the little riot that happened here instead. I don't know exactly what happened, but we'll get back to that on a later occasion. Now to the Financial Market Communication's proceedings in the Financial Committee of the Storting and the proceedings here in the Chamber today. The Communication is a Communication on the development and regulation of financial markets in 2011. Overall, the notification provides a very useful insight into both national and international financial markets. Fortunately, this case also shows that there is relatively broad consensus among the political parties on many of the important issues in the Norwegian financial market and its regulation. The Committee also shares a relatively good understanding that the aftermath of the financial crisis in 2008 and 2009 still characterises international financial markets and, to an even greater extent, real economic developments in many countries. Government finances are severely weakened in several places, millions of people are unemployed. There is considerable uncertainty about future developments. The situation is fragile. The concern that continued in financial markets in 2011 has also been a strong reminder that the Norwegian economy is also vulnerable to what is happening internationally. At the same time, the Committee notes that the Norwegian regulation of financial markets is fundamentally good and has proved robust in the context of the international financial crisis. Norway has taken advantage of the lessons learned from our own banking crisis 20 years ago. After the banking crisis, as we know, we made greater demands on banks, improved financial legislation, strengthened supervision and modernised the deposit guarantee scheme. Together with order in government finances, contributions included: the lessons learnt from the time when the Norwegian economy was saved for many of the worst results of the international financial crisis a few years ago. One thing is clear: Norway has proven much better through this economic crisis than many other countries. GDP has now grown in nine consecutive quarters, employment is increasing, and unemployment is low compared to many countries. But although much has been done, there is still much to be done in the financial market field. One of the reasons for this is that we have a financial industry internationally operating with concepts such as ‘financial innovation’ – many will call it creativity – which means that we are constantly pushing the boundaries of what kind of products and what kind of regulation does not catch up with these things until after. That's why there's been a number of things done here at home in the last few years. We've tightened up the rules for bonuses in the financial sector. It was unpopular among many, but it was true, because it was largely an incentive for excessive risk-taking among many actors who had their wages directly linked to the level of risk taken. The legislation on so-called structured savings products has been tightened up. It is currently in practice a ban on sales to individuals. This really took too much time. Those who were supposed to take care of this did not do their job to a large enough extent, and many things are now in the justice system. But new rules have come into place, and hopefully we've cleaned this up, so that fewer people get into trouble later. Action has been taken to strengthen the consumer side of the Financial Supervisory Authority. Furthermore, tomorrow in the Grand Duchy, we have a proposal for a representative on the subject of the Financial Complaint, in which a broad majority goes in to see if it is possible to see whether it can also be organised even more efficiently and better for consumers than it is today. So we continue to strengthen the regulatory framework around the industry, and that's good. There are many things that can be said about the evolution that will now come. There are many new regulations going on. A majority of the Committee subscribes to the government's willingness to exploit the national space of action we have to create a framework for banks and financial institutions in Norway, which enables us to stand firmer than the minimum requirements imposed by the EU and EEA systems. That's good. It has turned out before that it has been very successful. In addition, there is already a discussion about the financial industry in Norway and internationally should contribute more to the community through increased taxation — for example, in Norway. as in many other countries, financial services sold via the financial industry are in practice exempt from VAT — and it supports a majority, including the Christian People's Party, about it. But we see a much more waiting attitude from the Right, the Advance Party and the Left. I think it's a little surprising. I thought we in this room agreed that it is important that all industries, regardless of what they sell, contribute to a large enough extent. Now that we have a system — perhaps in a few years’ time — that can help balance this, I believe Norway should also use the space for action. With this, I recommend – this time – the correct setting. I'm sure you'll regret the confusion.
ParlaMint-NO_2012-06-12.ana.ud2618e4134	There is a great deal of agreement about a lot of things in this case. I think that's positive. It is important that there is a clear political signal for such an important industry, and it is also important that there is a consensus on reality in this area. The financial and banking industries are a central part of a well-functioning economy. It is important for a modern economy to have a credit market that actually works, that has confidence among people, and that has confidence among investors. It allows for safe saving and efficient lending opportunities. Again, it is important for many people to realize their housing dreams, their dreams of their own car, that business can have opportunities to invest in business both locally and globally, and for people with money in their hands it is possible to save for the future. All of this is important in order for us to have the best possible experience. In recent years, we have experienced unrest in financial markets. We've had a debt bubble, and we've had a housing bubble. It first resulted in a financial crisis and now in a debt crisis. It's probably because we've experienced a distance between the monetary values that have been out there and the real values that have been on the ground. So it's believed that things were worth more in dollars and dollars, or dollars, pounds, depending on what currency you want to use, than what the market is actually willing to pay for it today. Access to cheap credit has been one of these things, but there are also other reasons. We now experience a tough period, where this huge debt that has been accumulated is to be rebalanced, so that the values that are at the bottom correspond to the monetary values that you are willing to pay for. There is a rather heavy and dramatic fight going on in many countries that lenders see that they will lose money, borrowers will lose money, or lose the real values they are sitting on, and in many countries taxpayers will also lose some money. This situation is not uniform. There are different situations in most countries. In some countries, the banking system has been collapsing, which has made taxpayers pay a price; in others, politicians themselves have been irresponsible. This has happened across policy lines and across geography. But this conflict we will experience that it continues to play between the different stakeholders in a society that, of course, wants to lose as much as possible and apply it to others instead. Then we hope that the crisis packages with which the EU is now working – at the latest yesterday when it was Spain, but earlier for Greece – will be as good as possible and will have confidence in the market that will restore peace, and one can begin to focus on the future and new investments rather than on how to balance old losses. The reasons for the bias are, as I mentioned, access to cheap credit, but there are also partial political decisions in some countries that have made this work out, by wishing that people who do not have the ability to pay would still be housing owners. By shifting the repayment period and having only interest payments, people have taken up more loans than they would actually serve when the time to pay off arose. In some countries, there is also the possibility of going away from all debts by going from it an owner – as in the United States: if you go from the house, you don't have to take the debt with you. It makes it more interesting to gamble in a market – one doesn't want to go down on its own. The only thing you lose is the money you may have paid in the meantime. In Norway, this is quite different. I think that's also good -- it makes you think twice before taking up big loans, because the debt will last for a while. Then we have to make sure that it doesn't mean that you're in a debt situation for the rest of your life if it goes wrong. In other countries, there is a market that has come out crooked, a market that has deliberately or unconsciously abused regulatory gaps, and which means that political regulation must be better than it has been. All in all, this means that financial products have been mispriced. There has been a lack of transparency. This has resulted in good and bad loans being mixed together, and people have paid for these loans as if they were all good. Thus, a priced risk is wrong. It is an important task for political authorities to contribute to its failure to do so. We must help to ensure transparency in the market, so that people make decisions based on a real basis and not on a misguided basis. Another area where policy has failed is that of the EU, where a single currency has been introduced, which has enabled political authorities in some countries to borrow at a much lower rate than they have previously received. In some southern European countries in particular, the money press has been used to pay for previous loans. Thus, a high inflation rate has been observed, but also high interest rates. Because interest rates have been so high, people have been willing to borrow money to these countries, because it's the real interest rate at the end. This old recipe doesn't work when you're in a euro-cooperation, because then you can no longer print your own money. But one has had very good access to cheap credit because one has had an EU interest rate base instead of individual national interest rates. Thus, many of these countries have taken up large loans, but when they have to pay back, they do not have the opportunity to print money and remove it by inflation. This results in a global debt and financial crisis – for various reasons, but it still affects us all. The situation we are now experiencing underlines the importance of having predictable and good market regulation. It is important both for customers and for banks, and it is important that that regulation ensures trust. The Progress Party will warn against saying that things are going so well in Norway. In the previous debate, one stressed that the oil industry is one of the main reasons why Norway is doing well. We must avoid the illusion that Norway is now going so well that we can start tightening up individual industries and believe that they will still be doing well. We need to ensure that the industries in Norway have good competitive conditions against their competitors abroad. We must avoid treating Norwegian institutions worse than their competitors. It is unfortunate that the signals given in this case concerning stricter regulation of Norwegian banks compared to foreign banks, not least because many Norwegian banks compete directly with their Scandinavian friends in the Norwegian market. Swedish and Danish banks have approx. 30 pst. market share, and it goes without saying that if Swedish and Danish banks can raise capital cheaper and simpler than Norwegians, they will be able to offer loans to Norwegian customers in Norway cheaper than their Norwegian competitors. It's not lucky. We must ensure that the risk weight and the capital adequacy requirements are coordinated across borders. In what the government is now adding, I fear that Norwegian banks will pay a higher price in the market. This means that their margins have to be reduced if they are to maintain their market share, and it is in the long run that they will have a lower competitive situation. In the long term, banks also live on the margins they have on their loans — deposits and loans — and then we must not destroy Norwegian banks so that they lose the margins they could have. However, let me stress that the banks have a heavy responsibility to act responsibly, both in terms of ‘normal’ loans for housing and cars, and in terms of the sale of financial products. Especially lately, there have been some issues around. The sum for the banks is that good regulation and supervision are part of the whole thing that they have to live with. I'm not arguing that we should get rid of it, but we need to ensure equivalence and equal conditions with the competing countries. It is important for competition, but regulation is also important, as I said, because the impact of a collapse in banking affects society in general and can affect taxpayers and the state budget. However, let me say that we are skeptical about the way the Financial Supervisory Authority has changed its policy in the last year. They have in practice tightened their housing lending policy. That they now require one to have 15 pst. equity, we consider unfortunate. Many suggest that, across what was the objective, we have had inappropriate results. What we see is that as long as the Financial Supervisory Authority regulates residential loans thoroughly, but consumer loans much less thoroughly, we get a spillover effect, because some young customers go into the consumer loan market and get the equity they need. It's more expensive loans, they're less regulated, and there's a lower customer relationship between the borrower and the lender. Therefore, it would have been an advantage if the banks could behave as before, carefully evaluate the individual, look at the ability to pay and lend when they thought it was responsible. It is especially when the House Bank can stand on the outside of the Finansinspektionen's domain and provide the loans that private banks cannot. The progress party has no faith that the Husbank is better suited than usual, private banks to assess the credit and the ability to pay the individual. What we need in this matter is a new housing policy, not a new policy from the Financial Supervisory Authority. We need to ensure better access to land, better regulations that are less demanding, and a better BSU system. It is not the Financial Supervisory Authority that is responsible for managing housing policy in Norway, as it is now. It should have been big business and government. We from the Progress Party find – but on behalf of all the opposition parties, I believe – that despite too many contributions from the Storting through proposals, today's government has not been willing to make a single change of relevance to improve the housing market. And there must be a way of how long a government should be able to sit, before it promotes a housing report for the Storting. They know that the rental market will increase as a result of the failure of young people at the start-up stage to enter this market. This actually worsens the situation – giving the opposite effect. The housing companies have good times because the rental prices go up and the interest rate is still low. It actually makes it even more expensive for young first-time settlers, who are forced to sit on the outside. They are not allowed to save in owner-occupied dwellings at a record interest rate, but must enter a rental market which, relatively speaking, is record expensive. It will eat of the equity that they should actually use to pay down on the owner-occupancy. So, about complicated savings products. There's more cases going on in court, and we're not going to sue here. People shouldn't buy products that they don't understand. It should be a rule of thumb. When people come to a bank and are offered a product that they don't fully understand, the spinal reflex should be that they say no thanks. At the same time, we know that there have been cases where eager bankers have led people to believe that the risk is less than it is, that the product is simpler than it is. One sees that people pledge both the house and the car to buy the product that seems too good to be true. And that's usually the same thing. That's why it's important that we intervene and intervene with the unprofessional actors, who trick people into buying products and taking a risk far greater than what they themselves are aware of. The work of the Christian People's Party in its proposal for a representative – although the Progress Party does not support all the proposals – must continue, once again because it is important to ensure confidence in the market. Finally, Norway is doing very well. The oil industry has tripled its investment in ten years. This should say a little about how much we should thank the oil industry, but also about what concerns we should have if the oil price goes down and oil investments stop – what consequences it will have for the Norwegian economy. Then I think we'll see that much of what's happened in Norway in recent years is not about red-green politics, but about oil prices. The government should be a little more ground-based and reality-oriented about what are the real causes of today's situation, rather than making this a policy. It'll be an ever-increasing debt pressure. That means that if interest rates start to rise, while the oil price goes down, it'll be harder for people to serve this debt. We must then be more effective at stimulating business and other economic activity in Norway – not to say that Norway should always go ahead because we can afford it, and because we have stricter regulations, stricter laws, than our competitors have. We would like to point out that the government, through both the Financial Market Communication and the international work being done, and the national proposals that are being consulted, have a wide range of proposals that are suitable for ensuring financial stability. There we share the government’s goal of securing a strong, robust and secure banking market – of course you do. At the same time, however, there is a question of the extent to which specific measures can be taken and whether the consequences of such specific measures have been discussed. The right considers it important that banks build soundness in order to meet stricter capital requirements and become more robust and have supported government requests for, inter alia. reduced dividends to contribute to increased equity. However, when the government additionally announces increased payments to Bank Insurance Funds, considers introducing increased taxes and levies on profits and salaries, considers taxing added value generated in the financial sector and introducing countercyclical capital buffers, as well as reduced dividends and special-purpose capital adequacy requirements, the Right is committed to asking the government to also ensure that there is a balance between the requirements and the ability of banks to finance Norwegian business. What we're not missing is that the financial institutions are also the lifeblood of society. We want to live with ups and downs in society, and regulations are always a balance between creating security for development and the ability to finance future economic growth. It is a difficult balance, it must all be recognized, and it is seen in those degrees downwards in Europe. A little to what's happening internationally: in many ways you can see indications that what's happening in Europe now is that the road to hell is paved with good intentions. I think everyone wants a secure financing system, but what we see now is that it has been a huge concentration. There's been a drive towards larger units in the system, and all of a sudden you have institutions that are so big that they fall under the term 'too big to fail', that is, they'll tear the whole community apart if they start to stay together. It's a huge challenge that I haven't seen any very clear solutions to, and I don't see a quick fix here either. That's why I initially said that I think this is a debate we're going to have for many years. But what surprises me is that, in Europe, the focus is now more on banks not having to end than on maintaining competition in the financial sector. There's no doubt that many of the problems we have now are driven by the fact that we've got institutions that are so big and so dominant that the social system doesn't tolerate them going beyond the end. The paradox is that on the day the institution has grown so large, it is perceived to have State guarantees out in the loan market, and receives significantly cheaper funding than, for example, the small savings bank that exists in some valley in Norway, as there is not a problem at all. Of course, it's a local problem, but it's not system-critical if such a small savings bank is going to end up. Here, in fact, I think the long-term solution is to focus on the trend that the whole market has to drive so big units that society is at risk of breaking up with them. Then we have a really big problem. The right financial fraction was a trip in London and looked at the concentration there. The four-five largest institutions in England now have administrative capital equal to 80 pst. of the market and they have administrative capital equal to four times GDP. It goes without saying that there is a very big challenge here that we are not going to regulate nationally, but that we should go into close international cooperation to find good solutions. Because, as I said, the financial industry is also the lifeblood of society, and without a well-functioning financial system, we will weaken society's capacity for growth and development. At least we can't afford that in today's situation.

ParlaMint-NO_2012-06-12.ana.ud2618e4405	Good consumer protection is essential for financial stability and an important objective in itself in order to achieve a well-functioning financial market. In the years leading up to the financial crisis in 2008, attention on consumer protection was too poor, and many people were injured by, for example, investing. in compound savings products for borrowed money. After the financial crisis, many financial products are no longer on sale and it has been made clear to the Financial Supervisory Authority how significant consumer protection is. The Council of State has also recently sent out a proposal to clarify explicitly in the Financial Supervision Act that the Financial Supervision should ensure that institutions under supervision take account of the interests and rights of consumers. Such clarification in the legal text of the Consumer Liability of the Financial Supervisory Authority may make it clear that the pursuit of consumer interests remains, and shall be, a very important part of the role of the Financial Supervisory Authority. This I think is very positive. Tomorrow we shall be debating a proposal from the Christian People's Party, where we will also be on the same topic, but I would like to stress the importance of this area by including it today. We need a well-functioning financial market in our country. In order for us to have that, trust between financial institutions and customers must be good. It is essential that customers know who they are dealing with when they contact a bank, or even get an enquiry. It is therefore positive that it is now proposed that financial institutions in their marketing should not be able to use names or names covering which institution will be responsible for the customer relationship. In the time before the financial crisis in 2008, it was also a problem. A well-functioning Financial Supervision is important. We have a well-functioning supervision in the first place, which we can do even better by further strengthening consumer protection and thus trust in our financial institutions. It is essential that we have a functioning banking system in our country. Not least, it is important that we feel that the banks take on a social responsibility. In many communities around our country, savings banks are a major contributor to the development of local society through good and efficient banking activities and through part of the surplus being made available to donors in the form of grants to many teams, associations and individuals, which, through the funds, can contribute to more democratic development and value creation in small and large communities. It is the ownership of savings banks — historically they are self-owned — that enables these banks to share their profits for non-profit purposes rather than for shareholders, as in commercial banks. This is positive for communities. The volume and amount of the amount allocated to this type of purpose have increased in recent years. The current legislation and the practice of the Financial Supervisory Authority indicate that the concept of ‘useful purposes’ should be interpreted relatively widely. However, the savings bank association has expressed concern that there will be a narrower interpretation of the term ‘useful purpose’ when it is announced that in the forthcoming legislative proposal on financial corporate law there will be a concrete analysis of what is contained in the term ‘useful purpose’. ParlaMint-NO_2012-06-12.ana.ud2618e4485	In this debate we will discuss both Report. St. 17 for 2011–2012, on the management of the State pension fund, and Document 8:81 S for 2011–2012, on the proposal by Trine Skei Grande for the reduction of the State pension fund foreign investment in companies that destroy tropical forests. The paper on the management of State pension funds, SPU and SPN contains results and assessments of the management in 2011 and plans for the further development of the fund’s investment strategy in the future. In line with what was announced in last year’s communication, this Communication sets out plans to change the investment strategy in SPU in relation, inter alia, to:\t\t You can change the settings of the grid. \t\t \t new reference index for SPU bond investment with GDP weighting of government bonds and simplification of the index by the withdrawal of some sub-markets. Implementation of these changes has begun. Furthermore, it is envisaged to expand the reference index to include government bonds from emerging economies. new geographical distribution of SPU’s share investments that are based to a greater extent than before on market weights. It is set to reduce the proportion in Europe by about 10 percentage points. The share of emerging economies is increasing by about 2 percentage points. plans for a change in the system of rebalancing the share of the fund, which is also presented. The majority of the Committee endorses the changes that are now being made. Transparency in the management of the State pension fund and our ability to maintain a long-term investment strategy for the fund, including in periods of turmoil in the financial markets, are prerequisites for a broad trust in the management of the State pension fund. The ongoing reporting by the Ministry of Finance, Norway’s Bank and the Folketrugdfondet and Stortinget’s treatment of the annual notification of the State pension fund are an important part of this. As demonstrated by the Committee's position on the notification of the year, there is broad agreement on the overall long-term strategy and objective of the Fund. This is particularly important now, with the unrest and uncertainty in financial markets and with the sovereign debt crisis in Europe. In such a situation one might wonder whether our common savings are still placed safely in shares, bonds and property, which have been the strategy since the fund was created. If we look at history, this strategy and its development have served us well. The State petroleum fund, which was later renamed the State pension fund abroad, was established in 1990 and the first money transfer was made in 1996. The background was a broad political consensus that oil revenues should not only be used in the short term in each year’s budget, but should be used in the long term for the benefit of future generations to take part in oil wealth and for the sake of competitive industry. In 2001, the action rule was adopted, a rule that would ensure the long-term and planned use of oil revenues from the State budget, similar to the expected real return of the fund. In 2004, the Ethics Guidelines were introduced and the Ethics Council was established and, following the evaluation in 2009, a more comprehensive strategy for responsible investments was introduced. Looking at the results of this long-term oil resource management, it has served us mainly well. The annual return on SPU from 1998 to the present day has, however, varied between minus 23.3 pst. and 25,6 pst., and the return of SPN has varied between minus 25,2 pst. and 33.5 pst. In the active administration, too, the results achieved vary from year to year, stressing that we need to be prepared that the value of the State pension fund varies significantly from year to year. The figures from the Bank of Norway show that we have to calculate fluctuations in the value of the SPU from one year to the next on 10 pst. Corresponding figures from the Swedish Social Security Fund show annual fluctuations in the SPN value of nearly 17 pst. For 2011, both SPU and SPN have negative returns. The reason for this was weak development in the stock markets for both funds. The result in 2011 is marked by considerable uncertainty in financial markets in relation to the growth outlook for the world economy. The European sovereign debt crisis also had a negative impact on the level of risk that investors were willing to take. This was particularly reflected in the fall in the European stock market. However, the strategy and the objective have a long-term perspective of which there is broad political agreement, and over time both the Bank of Norway and the Swedish Social Fund have created added value in management. Since 1997, the average annual real return on investments in SPU has been 2.7 pst. This is lower than the Ministry's estimate of a long-term real return of 4 pst., but is still well within the uncertainty that we have to expect. We have an investment fund of 3 300 billion. kr by the turn of the year, and we have had good income to the state fund to build the welfare society we know today, from the foundation. There has also been attention and debate in the population and in the media on the management of the pension fund, both on SPU and on SPN. It's good that there's attention to the fund. In particular, it is the investment strategy and the concrete investments in relation to the ethical guidelines that create debate. There is, however, a great deal of dispersal in the debate. Some are concerned with the economic consequences of exclusion, which is part of the instruments for breach of the SPU ethical code. The notification points out that the costs of exclusion in isolation are estimated at approximately 10,6 billion. NOK lower returns in the period 2005-2011. Of these, exclusion from tobacco-producing companies amounts to $9.6 billion. kr, or 90 pst. of the lesser-yielding return. I would point out that exclusion from the fund is ethical and not financially motivated, and that such a political choice to exclude investment in tobacco may have such costs. On the other hand, short-term losses may prove to be profitable in the long term. There have also been several debates on concrete investment in relation to ethics and our foreign policy, with demands for more exclusions. That is what the draft representative we are discussing today is about. According to the proposer, the annual report to the State pension fund abroad indicates that the fund had invested at least 16 billion by the end of 2010. SEK in 38 companies involved in rainforest grazing. She believes that SPU is making a lot of money in industries responsible for the destruction of huge rainforest areas, such as logging, mining, certain types of oil extraction and the production of palm oil, cellulose and meat in rainforest areas. On this basis, the proposer proposes to investigate the impact of State pension funds abroad on deforestation in developing countries and how to improve the situation, to ensure the independence of the Ethics Council and to review all SPU investments in sectors where serious environmental damage is prevalent, in particular rainforest, and to ensure greater transparency. From the SPU to investing in shares in 1998 to today, much has been done in the development and focus on the ethical aspects of SPU management. The Ethics Guidelines were introduced in 2004 and evaluated in 2009, resulting in the introduction of a more comprehensive approach to responsible investment practices in 2010. This strategy targets both the work of the Ethics Council and the Bank of Norway. The work on responsible investments includes both international cooperation for the development of best practices, environmental investments, research and investigation, ownership and observation and exclusion of companies. SPU is a financial investor with investments in over 8 000 companies. As underlined in the letter from the Ministry of Finance to the case, investors generally do not add fresh money to the company when buying shares in the secondary market. In the case of such share purchases, the purchase price is due to the selling shareholder. Therefore, such an investment and increased activity cannot necessarily be equated with the company, so it may be difficult to assess whether SPU affects deforestation in developing countries. However, there are constant assessments that have led to the exclusion of 55 companies today due to breaches of the fund's ethical rules. Two of these were excluded in 2010 due to illegal logging and serious environmental damage in tropical rainforests. In addition, share purchases give voting rights and the ability to exercise ownership. Norway’s Bank has a climate as a focus area in its ownership exercise and has developed its own expectation document in this field. The exercise of ownership may in some cases contribute to a change in the behaviour of companies. With regard to the work of the Ethics Council, I would like to support what the Department says, namely that the Ethics Council is both formal and truly independent, and that the Council itself does its own priorities of its resources. The task of the Ethics Council is to advise the Ministry of Finance, and the scheme is based on independent testing by the Ministry of Finance. I think it is important to stress that the majority of the Committee supports this division of labour. At the same time, I would like to stress the importance of the Ethics Council contributing to identifying sectors or areas where the risk of breaches of the ethical guidelines is greatest, giving priority to resources to capture investments in companies that violate the ethical guidelines at the earliest opportunity. The Ethics Council’s annual report states that during the next few years they plan to review, inter alia: illegal logging and other particularly destructive logging, so that the mapping requested by the proposer is fully preserved. There is broad agreement that the SPU should be managed responsibly and that the Fund should have a strategy for responsible investment practices, but that the Fund should not be used as a foreign policy tool. The SPU is used in this area as a model of multiple investors and investment funds, including by the IMF and the OECD. On the Committee's travel abroad where we present the SPU and are presented to similar foreign investment funds, my impression is that the State pension fund abroad is one of the world's most open funds and a fund that other countries' political authorities and funds look to in terms of the management model chosen. Nevertheless, I would like to stress that today's model is not high in stone. The ethical guidelines, the investment strategy and the government pension fund management model have been developed over time and will also be further developed in the future. However, experience so far shows that we have the ability to facilitate the long-term and good management of the country's petroleum assets, so that it can benefit both current and future generations. With that, I recommend the Committee's position on the two issues we are now debating.

ParlaMint-NO_2012-06-12.ana.ud2618e4694	I would like to thank the President-in-Office for a good review. I am going to spend most of my speaking time on the proposal for a representative that the Left has promoted, to which the rapporteur also referred. Fortunately, a few days ago, agreement was reached in the climate agreement here in Stortinget. It is good that the list has been raised as to what should be the minimum of the measures to be implemented to reduce greenhouse gas emissions in the coming years. Nearly four and a half years ago, the Storting made similar settlements. One of the measures taken by the Left, Right and Christian People's Party at the time was that the Norwegian contribution to stopping deforestation in developing countries increased by about 3 billion. kr every year. Later, the government has tried to create the impression that it was the idea of government and government parties and has traveled the world and received medals and honours on behalf of this initiative. It is well and good that Norway is recognised for the important work we contribute to conserving rainforests. There is no doubt that it is crucial in a global climate perspective. The more strange it is that the State pension fund abroad, SPU, invests heavily in companies involved in building down and reducing rainforests. It'll be a form of double morality, which is regrettable. Although it is not that direct, it can actually be said that Norway’s contribution to the conservation of rainforests is financed through SPU’s investments in companies that contribute to the opposite. The Left considers this to be both unfortunate and regrettable. It also places Norway’s important commitment to the conservation of tropical forests in a strange light. The report ‘Beauty and the beast: Norway’s investments in rainforest protection and rainforest destruction’, published by the Rainforest Fund and the Norwegian Nature Conservation Association, shows that at the turn of the year 2011/2012, SPU had shares for nearly 82 billion. SEK in 73 companies in sectors where deforestation and other forest destruction are very common. Of course, not all of these investments are broken or suspected of breaching the ethical guidelines of the SPU, but it is almost impossible to get an overview of which companies break the guidelines and which companies operate acceptable. As an example of Norway’s double standards in this area, the report of the Rainforest Fund and the Nature Conservation Association points to Indonesia, where Norway has promised Indonesia up to 6 billion through bilateral agreements. kr to reduce the deforestation of the country. At the same time, SPU invests 3.8 billion. in 21 plantation companies in the palm oil industry, which is the main cause of deforestation in Southeast Asia. The Left has therefore put forward three proposals, which unfortunately only receive the support of the Christian People's Party when we come to voting later today. It is sad that SV does not even want to support the proposal for something as harmless as investigating SPU’s activities in relation to deforestation in developing countries. For the party which has both the Minister for the Environment and the Minister for Development – and which claims to be the birth aid for the ethical guidelines of the Oil Fund – this should be evident. I must be honest and say that there is something surprising about the technocratic tone that permeates the Minister of Finance's response to the Committee. One thing is that it is skeptical or incomprehensible to some of the formulations or claims made in the proposal, another is the claim that Norway contributes to deforestation through the SPU because: ‘does not add fresh money to the company when buying shares in the secondary market. In the case of such share purchases, the purchase price is due to the selling shareholder’. According to the Minister of Finance, it is very good to invest in companies that contribute to deforestation, because the money does not go directly to the company, but to sellers – who have taken the sense and sell themselves out of companies that do this type of business. On this basis, together with the wording of the Council of State in letters to the Financial Committee that he considers that: ‘It is not appropriate to allow Norway’s involvement in measures aimed at the conservation of rainforests to lead to a fundamentally different or more stringent treatment of companies operating in that sector’; there is reason to draw the conclusion that the Minister of Finance and the Government believes that it does not at all matter whether SPU invests in companies that operate underpinning Finland or in companies that cut tropical rainforests in Indonesia. For the Left, this plays a significant role. It plays a role in Norway’s commitments under the UN Climate Change Agreement, it plays a role in reducing global greenhouse gas emissions, and it plays a role in good ethics and morals.

ParlaMint-NO_2012-06-12.ana.ud2618e5002	Today, we have a proposal from the Progress Party to change the law to be adapted to international guidelines for amateur and professional boxing. The International Boxing Association has recently decided that no helmet boxing is permitted. Norway has banned professional boxing in Norway since 1981. In Europe, only Norway has a ban on professional boxing. Europe has a more stringent licensing system than the rest of the world, and there has been no death in Europe in professional boxing since 1985. The ban in Norway was thus introduced in 1981. The reason was the risk of medical adverse effects on the performer. Today, medical surveillance is strict, including in professional boxing. The Norwegian Boxing Association has thus, according to the proposers, been put in a difficult situation, which could lead to Norwegian amateur boxers not being given the opportunity to box in large international championships such as the World Cup, the Olympics and the European Championships, as a consequence of the fact that Norwegian boxers, under the Norwegian Boxing Association, do not have the opportunity to box without a helmet, either in Norway or abroad. As a result of the recent action taken by the International Linked Association, the Norwegian Board of Sports has proposed in its Decision of 3 May 2012 to repeal the Professional Boxing Act of 1981 and instead to make the knockout law applicable to professional boxing. The Decision repeals the distinction between amateur boxing and professional boxing, but does not change the legislation that boxing matches are subject to in Norway. The sports council has not taken a decision on any change to the regulation of the knockout law and writes in its opinion that the Norwegian Boxing Association itself must promote amendments to the Ministry of Culture, if they wish to change the regulation as a result of international regulatory changes in its sport. The Government has taken note of the decision of the sports board. The Government has also informed the Committee that it would like to establish a sample to look at the legal aspects of the possible merger of the Professional Boxing and Knockout Act. The selection will also examine the changes in the rules adopted by the International Boxing Association. The right considers Norwegian fighters must be given the same conditions as fighters in the rest of Europe. We believe that Stortinget should lift the ban and leave this to sport itself. We want the government to contribute to the same thing. With this, I address the proposal that the Right and the Advance Party have promoted in this matter.

ParlaMint-NO_2012-06-12.ana.ud2618e5024	Once again, professional boxing is the theme of this hall. This time, the reasons given by the proposers are that the International Boxing Association has decided that if men are not allowed to box with helmets, the impact on Norwegian boxers will be considerable, which will not be able to participate in future major championships. But in Appendix 1 of our case papers, the Council of State states that the Department has been in contact with AIBA, the International Boxing Association, which states that they have not taken a decision on this. However, in the International Boxing Association, there is a process to assess whether, for example: men over 18 years of age shall box without a helmet. The associated statement is that a new strategy for amateur boxing has been adopted as of 2013. I think that the proposers are positive for professional boxing. But the approach to changing the law, in my view, becomes a little bit helpful when they choose to interpret decisions in the International Boxing Association as they do, with regard to helmet bans. Furthermore, the proposers are anchoring their position by pointing out that in their decision of 3 May this year, the sports board proposes to repeal the law prohibiting professional boxing. Neither do they meet here. The sports council would not decide if they would like to open up professional boxing in Norway, and they believe professional boxing must be subject to the knockout law and its safety provisions if the law were to be lifted by today’s debate. It is important to point out that the sports council in its decision did not decide whether any changes to the safety provision of the knockout law should be made. The proposers also ask the government to propose amendments to the legislation adapted to international rules on amateur boxing. The sports council did not take a direct position on this proposal. The Norwegian Sports Association and the Olympic and Paralympic Committee consider it natural that it is the special association in question, the Norwegian Boxing Association, which itself initiates any changes to the safety regulations resulting from international regulatory changes, whether they should come. The majority backs this view. To this end, the majority of the Committee takes note of the decision of the sports board and proposes the establishment of a selection to look at the legal aspects of any merger of the law prohibiting professional boxing and knockout. They will also provide the sample to examine the possible changes in the rules proposed by the International Boxing Association. Whether you're for or against professional boxing is about feelings and facts. When it comes to elucidating such things, we have the good tradition here at Stortinget that we politicians get through hearings to shed light on difficult questions from different sides. It is reassuring in front of political decisions. In our hearing we got to meet Cecilia Brekkhus, a very skilled and through professional sportswoman, and we heard her views on why we should lift the decision against professional boxing here in the country. It is understandable that she is disappointed not to be able to show up in front of a Norwegian audience. In the same hearing we also got insight into the second part of the boxing, namely the medical one. The special thing about boxing is that the very purpose is to damage the opponent, while in other sports you do everything you can to prevent damage, and punish both negligent and deliberate damage. After the hearing, I was positive about lifting the boxing ban here in the country, and I must honestly admit that in the previous round it was hard to press the red button and say no to professional boxing in the country. Some may claim that after the previous treatment, I have been down for count, how the long-term effect of my boxing exposure has made me slow, but certainly began to think through the message from our leading medical experts in this area and what they said in the hearing. Today, my attitude towards professional boxing has turned from being too against, one and only because I believe in the medical expertise and what they say. It is confirmed every time I see a boxing match on TV, where you see in a slow film how the battle reproduces through your head. Some claim it's beautiful – I don't share that point of view anymore. Nor am I disappointed that we are perhaps one of the few countries in the world that have the legislation we have. In this case it is presented by the proposers that if Norwegian boxers do not get boxes without helmets, it would be problematic to be boxes in Norway. It's possible, but I don't believe it. Speaking of helmet: in my simplicity I have often wondered why there are rules to protect what is under the belt, while the head should be unprotected. It becomes to me a great paradox that repeated beatings during the seat belt cause one to be judged out of the fight, while the knockout on the head is performed as aesthetically and beautifully, even though the opponent at best is sent to dreamland and at worst over to eternity.
ParlaMint-NO_2012-06-12.ana.ud2618e5039	I'm going to admit that I'm getting pretty scared when politicians take on the role of ethics preachers and take the role of moral police over which of the individual's interests are good or bad, which should be legal or not. In this case, both concrete facts and statistics, medical claims and physical consequences are argued, but also with a happy mix of the subjective opinions of individual politicians on what sport implies, perception of what sport's purpose actually is, and, as the previous speaker himself said, a 'it's hard to look at, we don't like it, so we ban' attitudes. Although we are run by a left-wing government here in the country, I would assume that they still feel a certain distance from the governments we see in Cuba, North Korea and Iran. Then it's a little suspicious that it's the only countries in the world where we find other politicians who have the same thinking and attitude on this issue, like our government. When it comes to the medical argument, one sees clearly that many of the participants in the debate have chosen to rely on a little simple rhetoric such as that sport has a blow to its head, and therefore is dangerous. But then it is strange that both amateur boxing, judo, karate, kickboxing and other sports are legal. They have the same number of strokes, and they also kick their heads, and they're not talking about banning them. Furthermore, it can be seen that the main distinction between amateur and professional boxing today is the use of helmets. So we can assume that the main argument for the government is that you don't want to remove this helmet. That's in and of itself quite strange, because we know that 95 pst. by the kinetic energy of an actual kind is determined by the thickness of the glove and that it is only 5 pst. by the force of the impact determined by whether or not you have a helmet. Then I wonder if it's the last five percent that's heavy on the scale of whether this sport is dangerous enough to have or not. A very widely used argument in this debate is that the ban should apply due to the extent of the damage you see in the sport. Then I wonder if they also intend to ban the other sports that have a much greater damage in Norway today, for example. rugby. They have far more spinal injuries, head injuries and deaths in this sport in Norway than do professionals at international level. Does the government want this to be banned for that reason? As mentioned at the outset, there are also very many who are trying to argue from an ethical or moral perspective in this matter. My opinion is that this is a rather simplistic and disrespectful way of seeing the sport in relation to the many hundreds of skilled practitioners we have here in Norway. A French sociologist — who really had this position initially — wanted to get under the skin of this environment and investigate what the professional boxing environment actually meant. What he describes in his work, both through his experience and in over fifty interviews with people in and outside of this environment, is that there is an enormous amount of mutual respect between the practitioners, it is discipline, it is understanding one another, and there is a physical and mental strength on the performers that is extremely important. For the perpetrators themselves, the sport does not deal with violence or with the greatest possible impact on the head. This is about a sport, a craft that combines strength, strategy and technical skills at a high level. It takes years of training to master the many forms of this discipline. Simplifying this by saying that it's about hitting each other in the head, at best testifying about ignorance, ignorance, but also, unfortunately, a pre-judgment of a whole sport and hundreds of great sportsmen in Norway. We in the Progress Party propose legalizing pro boxing – not because we glorify violence, not because we want people to hurt themselves, not because battles against their heads are good, but because boxing in almost every country in the world is allowed, accepted and respected, because we know that the medical aspect is taken care of, and because we know and believe that the socialists' desperate desire to be moral judges against the individual's own choices and for what they want to do is wrong.

The decision of the Norwegian Sports Association of 3 May this year to propose the repeal of the Professional Boxer Act and instead subject to pro boxing knockout law. It is then important that we have impact assessments and that any legislative changes then need to be assessed against what health consequences the changes may have for the practitioners.
