It has so far been targeted at some types of higher education. If you go to these and only in educational places in Norway, you get a scholarship. Some have automatically received NOK 100000 because of the training they have chosen, although they have not chosen to establish themselves as artists, while those who have studied abroad have not been given the opportunity to apply for these scholarships, even though they have established themselves as independent artists in Norway. That's why we're now using this money in a much fairer way, and we're making a scholarship for newly educated artists anyway, whether they've studied in Norway or abroad. It's a searchable scholarship that's going to be more fair. There are many of the foreign education courses that hold a very high level, but it is also true that some people are involved in art training that is not eligible in the Loan Fund. I don't think anyone, either from the opposition or the government parties, thinks that there should be some automation in which places of study to be supported, and that there is something that you automatically get when you choose, no matter what kind of education it is. By that I don't want to say that the rules of the Loan Fund are perfect in any case, and that in any case you have found the right education to be supported, because sometimes it's not right to get support from the State Loan Fund for education even if you've been involved in an art education abroad. Not all of them are keeping the right level. We also need to be aware of that. I'm happy for everyone who takes art education, but the competition is now much harder than before. When we made guarantee income in the 1970s, the idea was that everyone who trained as an artist would automatically receive income from the state. But when we do an education now and see what the newly educated in the visual arts field are doing, 70 pst. after five years with something next to it. That's also the reality they're going to go into. We want to have a dialogue with art education about how to better facilitate the start of your own business, your own entrepreneurship, when you finish your education. So the question of en route scholarships. This is primarily the responsibility of the Ministry of Education, as it is the case in Norway. It's not like that in Denmark. There is no responsibility of the Ministry of Knowledge for the arts education, which lies below the Ministry of Culture. However, in Norway there is a distinction between the Ministry of Education and the Ministry of Knowledge on the one hand and the Ministry of Culture on the other. As far as our scholarship schemes are concerned, they are aimed at newly educated artists. On the other hand, we have some very good schemes, the Dream Grant, which are kind of stimuli. But this money doesn't give enough money to take on an education abroad. It is a good initiative, but there is a lack of concreteity. We will be working towards the Cultural Promise III, looking at various forms of talent development that can help us to raise talents from all parts of the population. One concrete example is what the Norwegian Theatre is doing. If you look at those who attend theatre education/theatre folk college, almost no one has a minority background. Now they're making their own school to get more from our minorities up on stage, and it's a very good initiative that just helps to raise artists from the whole population.

Andreassen raised the matter was an initiative of the Ofoten Nutrition Council and the Chairman of the Board of Directors Alf Martin Solvin. Business has always been behind the project, supporting it with lobbying, investigation, good words and resources. There's a broad consensus locally. It has been absolutely essential that we adopt the project here today in Stortinget. At the end of the 1980s there was a tunnel under or a new bridge over the Rombaksfjord. Already then, it was realized that toll financing was absolutely necessary to achieve such a large project. Cooperation with the State Road Administration and Narvik Municipality resulted in a consensus in 1996 to set aside the tunnel option and explore two alternatives. One was a new bridge, and the other was upgrading the road around the fjord – with a long tunnel from the north side of the Rombaken to Gésvik. Possibilities analyses carried out by the municipality in 1997 and 1998 are also key milestones. These documented land resources on Island soil and a significant toll potential at new bridges. It is a patient and nine-time work from business and politicians in Narvik and centrally – such as Tor Arne Ström, Anne Marit Bjørnflaten, Torstein Rudihagen and, not least, former director of the Bruges Company, Kjell Opseth – that has now resulted in the Brua finally being built. In the processes that have been carried out around this development at local and county level, it has always been assumed that the city airport in Framneslia will be shut down when the bridge is open to traffic. I am very pleased that these assumptions are followed up by the Ministry of Transport and are the basis for the decisions the Storting is making today. When the bridge is in place, there will be an acceptable travel time from the city centre to the airport at approximately. Forty minutes. This will mean major development opportunities for the Harstad/Narvik parent aerodrome, Evenes. It could mean more departures both northwards and southwards. Brua will also mean opportunities for new areas for both business and housing in Øyjord, a part of the city that is now only a bridge away from the centre. Brua will not least improve the transport efficiency of goods in the north. Narvik is the hub of goods in and out of the country, via road and train, and eventually via sea and trains to and from Narvik. Hålogalandsbrua, several crossing tracks and later double tracks on Ofotbanen are therefore important and correct north-eastern action. In addition, some, perhaps optimistic souls, have suggested that during the construction of the bridge some tourists will find it interesting to visit the construction site. Yes, it has even been suggested that this should be something the television companies could be interested in creating a reality series about. Architecturally, this becomes a great construction, in all modesty, in a natural environment. I will end by making the words of former Minister of Transport Liv Signe Navarsete my: The project Hålogalandsbrua is one of the foremost symbols of how we better connect the country. And to those who say that building bridges in the districts is bad social economy, I would say that Hålogalandsbrua is actually one of the most socio-economically profitable projects we have in our national transport plan. In addition, 18 km savings will deliver significant environmental benefits on one of the most traffic-laden roads in North Norway. Narvik, like several representatives, is one of the busiest logistics hubs in the north, where road and rail are connected. This generates extensive freight and commercial traffic, which is in addition to the use of the road by the rest of the population. The establishment of several large wholesale warehouses in Narvik at the end of Ofotbanen means that increasing volumes of freight are transferred from trains to a rapidly increasing number of trailers, moving further northwards and westwards along E6 and E10. The E6 from Narvik and northwards and the E10 towards Sør-Troms and Lofoten are now also the main trajectories for the export of large quantities of fish from the fishery and aquaculture industries. Good availability is essential for fresh fish to reach their markets in time. From the right, we will also strongly emphasise the road project Hålogalandbrua as a road safety project. Today's road from Narvik towards Bjerkvik is very variable, with severe driving conditions winter time and with a traffic load far beyond the one for which the road was once dimensioned. And the road has unfortunately accumulated a very gloomy accident statistics, with many lost lives and many serious injuries – losses and injuries that cannot be measured in money. It is therefore an undeniable fact that improved road standard experience means saved life and fewer damage. The new route of supply bridge will provide a modern, high-quality road and therefore will provide us with great human benefits as well as the environmental benefit of fewer kilometres and reduced emissions from significant heavy goods transport. I've had the pleasure of following the work with Hålogalandsbrua from its very beginning. Arnesen – who have the floor, one would think that it was not known that it was in this room that funding was determined. You can't blame others. It is we, through the votes here, who decide whether we should have tolls, or whether we should use the huge surplus we have to finance roads in Norway. I heard Hansen say that he was very pleased that in both Narvik and here one thought new. If Hansen wants to think new, you might start financing roads without tolls, because that had to be something new. I wonder many times why you wanted to go to Stortinget. They took part in election campaigns in Northern Ireland and fought to get into Stortinget. Was it not just that the decision to build roads could be made in this room, with the money we have in Norway. So we're going to spend 960 million. SEK to pay interest to finance a loan of 860 million. kr. Where's the sense of that? What's even worse is that the Norwegian state borrows money to Germany at 1.8 pst. interest to build roads, while in this country we have to borrow money for 6.5 pst. interest on road construction. It's something that's tearing wrong.
